The Belgravian

February 1995

The Belgravian

The official gazette of Belgrave Harriers

Number 222

Front page: ... And to think that we used to know him as "little Marlon". Shown here at the McDonald's YA League at Peterborough matching strides out of the blocks with Blackheath's Mark Findlay, Marlon Dickson had a great 1994 season. At 15 years of age he set personal bests of 11.36 and 23.1 and ran anchor leg for our U17M record breaking 4x100 quartette. He looks set to make a name for himself in 1995 when he remains in the same age-group and has already clocked 7.1 for an indoor 60m at the 'Palace'. Each season so far he has chopped 4/10ths off his 100m best. Another improvement like that would give him 10.96. Phil Goedluck's U17M record? - 10.96! This and other photos by Alan Mead unless otherwise stated.

Marlon DicksonMark FindlayPhil GoedluckAlan Mead

Feature · page 2

Tilda Rice Sponsorship Double

Leading rice specialists, Tilda Rice, are to be the official club sponsor for the Belgrave Harriers. With over 20 Athletes of International standard, Belgrave has, over the last three years, provided more Athletes for major Championships than any other British Club. Tilda will be supporting the Belgrave Harriers at a grass roots level through involvement with a special 'Sports-Link' scheme being set up for schools by the club. Additional funding is also being supplied by Government run 'Sportsmatch Award Scheme' which will match Tilda's sponsorship pound for pound. The proposed programme will provide basic fitness training for secondary school children outside school hours with the aim of increasing participation in sports in general and athletics in particular. Fifteen secondary schools in the London Borough of Wandsworth are set to benefit from this alliance which will include initial coaching, pupil and school awards, grading schemes, and after year 1, a full inter-schools Athletics League. Club members and visitors will soon see the Tilda Rice logo adorning athletics vests and on the perimeter advertising hoardings to mark the partnership. As part of a double sponsorship arrangement, Tilda Rice has also announced its sponsorship of 400m star Du'aine Ladejo in the year that he aims for a World Championships Medal. Tilda Rice is Du'aine's Vest Sponsor and in return, Du'aine along with the Belgrave Harriers will be helping to raise awareness of Tilda rice as a delicious, versatile energy food, a must for the modern athlete. The Tilda logo will feature on the Belgrave Harriers teams vests as they compete at major sports events this season and Du'aine will be carrying out a number of public appearances for the brand including a personal appearance on Tilda's stand at the 1995 International Food Exhibition at Earls Court in April and special celebrity role at the annual Tilda Rice 'Fun Run' held close to the company's offices in Rainham, Essex. Commenting on their Sponsorship activities, Kay McCarthy of Tilda Rice said, "We are proud to be involved with the Belgrave Harriers and Du'aine Ladejo - more and more people are looking to rice as a versatile energy food and we believe that our new association with the rising stars of track and field will further enhance the image of rice in all its forms and varieties." The new sponsorship means that club members can look forward to experiencing rice at its very best. Tilda will be introducing the complete range of Tilda rices and will be highlighting the convenience of rice - the 90's complex carbohydrate, an excellent energy food for athletes. Look out for a variety of exciting and wholesome rice meal ideas which will appear in regular recipe slots in The Belgravian magazine and 'rice parties' prior to Club Events. TRY THIS TILDA RICE RECIPE -

~ Perfectly Easy Pilaff ~

Full of high energy, complex carbohydrate, Tilda Rice and lean chicken combine to make this high protein, low fat dish that anyone can put together whether they can cook or not! 25g/8oz TILDA Basmati or American Long Grain Rice 2 tablespoons olive oil 250g/8oz skinless, boneless chicken breast cut in small pieces 1 medium onion, chopped 1 small red or green pepper, seeded & chopped 2 cloves garlic, crushed 2 teaspoons mild curry powder 1/4 teaspoon dried thyme 600 ml/1 pint stock 1 tablespoon mango chutney 2-3 tablespoons chopped fresh parsley (optional) 3 tablespoons natural yoghurt, to serve salt and ground black pepper

1) Heat the oil in a large saucepan and brown the chicken chunks for about 2 minutes. Add the onion, peppers and garlic and cook, stirring for another three minutes. 2) Stir in the Tilda Rice, cook for a minute then mix in the curry powder and thyme. 3) Pour in the stock, add the chutney and seasoning to taste then bring to the boil, stirring. Cover, turn down to a gentle simmer and cook for about 10 minutes if using Basmati, 12 minutes if using Long Grain. Allow to stand for 5 minutes before forking through the fresh herbs and serving topped with dollops of yoghurt.

Serves 2-3

Vegetarian Option: Replace chicken with 420g/10oz can of chick peas and lentils.

Du'aine LadejoKay McCarthy

Editorial · page 3

Editorial

It's been a long time ... The Belgravian is an expensive item and with Club finances stretched to a point that was more than comfortable in 1994, we had to keep to our promise to the Hon. Treasurer to keep to just the one issue during the Club year. We then planned for a Christmas-time publication date, but with the promise of great news to be announced just around the corner, a further delay was well worthwhile. We hope you enjoy the final result. Before we go any further we must wholeheartedly thank those members who contributed towards the magazine fund with their annual subscription. Without their support, production of a magazine in this format would be impossible.

These are very exciting times but sadly our delight in moving the Club forward has been tempered by the loss of some truly great Belgravians. In 1994 we said farewell to Micky Pyer, a great champion in his prime, who went on to become President of our Club and our longest ever serving member. Joe Scamell and Len Marchant were also staunch Belgravians; Joe's family continues to be represented on the walking scene through son Mike and although we had not seen much of Len recently, his exploits were still remembered and talked of by the older runners. Then just before Christmas came the terrible news that Tom Carter had died in his sleep while in hospital due to a chest infection. At the present time it seems impossible to accept that this greatest ever supporter of our Club will not be around any more. Appreciation of these men is expressed at greater length in the pages of this magazine but we also bring news of the death of Norman Dewberry, our oldest member at 92 years of age. Norman died early in January. Just a week or two beforehand when one of our number called in to see him he had been mending a puncture on his bicycle. A tribute to Norman will appear in the next issue of The Belgravian.

To repeat: these are very exciting times. Those who are involved in driving the Club forward know much effort is being expended to bring our hopes and plans to fruition but one sometimes wonders just how much the general member is aware of these strivings. Now, then, is the time broadcast some of our achievements and targets.

Our new Partners - Tilda Rice

Above all else we are delighted to announce a partnership between Tilda Rice and Belgrave Harriers. Tilda had already made plans to sponsor our European Champion Du'aine Ladejo but thanks to a coming together of Alison Thorne-Enwezoh of Quiet Storm Associates, who is managing Du'aine's affairs, Kay McCarthy of Tilda, and members of our own management team we can now announce a major sponsorship of our Club. Tilda Rice are a family firm based in Essex and have already had a little to do with athletics in the way of sponsorship of a "fun-run". You may have noticed their advertisements on television continued on following page ...

but now they are looking to promote their name and products while also assisting grass roots athletics and sport in the community. Worthy aims indeed. Tilda Rice will be sponsoring Belgrave Harriers for a fixed sum of £20,000 over a two year period and it is anticipated that we should be able to double this figure thanks to the Government's Sports Match Scheme. The sponsorship will be administered within our Club by our Finance Management Committee, a group made up of representatives from all sections of the Club, who will place their recommendations before the main Belgrave Harriers' Committee for final approval. For our part in this union Tilda will be acknowledged as our "Official Club Sponsor" on all Club and public promotional material including banners, letter headings, etc. The Tilda Rice logo will be incorporated within our club kit which will remain in the traditional colours of claret and gold. The company will be providing us with at least six mobile banners which all of our teams will take with them to display wherever they compete - women's and men's Southern League teams, cross-country teams, McDonald's YA teams, Walking, British League, Rosenheim, Thameside, the lot! They will also be providing self supporting hoardings which we will display at our home matches. Individually our members will, wherever possible, wear Tilda branded clothing at all interviews, press conferences and other public appearances.

We said it was exciting but there is more. As we will be wearing the Tilda brand on our racing strip (in strict accordance with IAAF/BAF rules) Quiet Storm Associates are also placing the case for re-kitting our teams with a well known sports clothing manufacturers who are currently showing an interest in the project.

While all this has been going on we have also been progressing elsewhere. We are able to announce a major initiative with the launching of the Belgrave Harriers' Youth Scheme. The intention is that it covers all the competitive sections of the Club. Over 50 schools have been circulated and made aware of our coaching scheme for young girls and boys which now takes in Battersea, Crystal Palace, Kingsmeadow and other venues as far south as Folkestone! We intend to promote an inter-schools track & field meeting at Crystal Palace in May while continuing with our existing schools league in a different format. We have stated our aim that we would like to place a girls' team in the McDonald's League. We are aiming to give our McDonald's teams a strong identity by awarding a team sweatshirt to each athlete scoring for us in the league - and doing something similar for our Thameside League teams. Participation in indoor athletics is being helped along by the double-decker bussing of athletes to the National Indoor Arena - at negligible cost to the Club - and we are appointing a coordinator to ensure that we provide massive turn-outs in the County and area track & field championships. Our road and cross-country people are also getting involved in this area and if there is any other field that you believe we should explore then get in touch with our Youth Development Scheme Director John Powell or any other Club Officer ... and by the way, we do need your commitment!

What else is going on? Let's just list a few items:

• There is the possibility that our Club Championships, while keeping broadly to last year's successful format, will also include an element of international competition for young athletes (by city as opposed to country). • We are planning to raise the profile of our Belgrave Open 10k and Half-Marathon road races. • We will be installing central heating in Belgrave Hall - soon. • We have published a Club Rule Book again - for the first time in years. • We are looking at the production of new Club clothing items - superior quality Belgrave casual wear sweat tops, bow ties ... • We have produced a brochure/poster advertising ourselves and our facilities in an aim to boost membership. • And while on the subject of membership we have set ourselves a target of 1,000 members by next year. • One scheme for installing a proper gymnasium at Belgrave Hall has been received and examined and we are now measuring it against other options to achieve the same.

Athletics in Belgrave Harriers is alive and well!

Micky PyerJoe ScamellMike ScamellLen MarchantTom CarterNorman DewberryDu'aine LadejoAlison Thorne-EnwezohKay McCarthyJohn Powell

Feature · page 5

From the outside, looking in

Abi Ekoku

How does the public view our sport after the latest round of revelations regarding alleged drug abuse in athletics? Abi Ekoku highlights some of the issues.

The image that British Athletics is not beset with the alleged drug problems of many other nations has been shattered by the revelations of the past few months from Helsinki and Victoria. Public perception/appreciation of athletic performance is very tainted at present, and the all-time low the sport achieved following the 1988 Ben Johnson affair in Seoul has been closely matched by the recent close media scrutiny of British T&F. The media do not need much encouragement to promote the negative (often interesting) sides of the sport. This trend is best seen prior to major championship events, where the sport is constantly maligned through numerous documentaries highlighting the issue of drugs. The diversity of opinion surrounding this issue is a feature that whilst promoting stimulating discussion, often adds to the confusion and cynicism of those who merely enjoy the spectacle of T&F.

Contrasting views

Such diversity of opinion is not restricted to those viewing the sport from differing perspectives - coaches/administrators/spectators/athletes. Two ex-world record holders, both middle-distance runners, both having competed internationally (mid '70s to mid '80s), both now TV pundits/analysts, have recently voiced completely contrasting views on the issue of drug-taking! Dave Moorcroft feels that despite the current climate, the sport is far 'cleaner' now than it was 15 years ago. The increased frequency and stringency of testing procedures and processes is the reason given for the recent rise in positive tests. Conversely, Steve Ovett maintains that the current level of cheating is greater now than at any other time in history. This he believes is largely down to greater financial rewards, and the increased pressure on athletes to produce high level performances with far greater regularity on an ever more congested calendar. Having conducted a previous study in this area, my view lends itself more to the Moorcroft school of thought. Many I have spoken to regarding Ovett's opinion view it as being very blinkered. Whilst money is an obvious motivating factor in the cheating stakes, the will to win, the glory, acclaim, affection, all rank at least equal to this. Hence the prominence of cheating long before large financial rewards could be accrued from the sport. Indeed, I wonder how anybody regularly travelling and competing between 1970 and 1988 can compare these two periods in the sport. The ease with which many British and foreign athletes openly flouted the laws of the sport in that period are very well known. This notwithstanding, T&F is far too often cast as the villain of international sport. In reality, it is one of the few sports to have seriously tackled the issue of drugs, and unfortunately has only achieved notoriety as a result of its high profile revelations over the past decade. Those interested need only analyse the statistics of those tested against positive results to see the unfair image the sport has created for itself.

Badge of dishonour

However, the image of British athletics still has that badge of dishonour pinned to its vest! Solomon Wariso and Colin Mackenzie's minor indiscretions have been widely publicised, the onus is still on Diane Modahl to clear her name (many have tried, but few succeed), while Paul Edwards has recently decided against what would have been a very futile appeal. Luckily these revelations came near the end rather than the beginning of a very important season! Innuendo and suspicion has long been prevalent in T&F. When athletes suspect each other it is rarely based on hard fact, but when accusations are levelled from media, public, et al, it becomes even harder to swallow. Rapid improvement is rarely credited to honest sweat and toil due to this constant cynical cloud. The late Cliff Temple in 1988 encapsulated in a single statement what many now unfortunately believe,

"The age of innocence is truly over, and cynicism is here to cloud our joy."

The time has come for athletes (past and present), the athletic authorities, and the athletics press to cultivate a new image for what I truly believe to be the 'purest' sport of all.

Abi EkokuBen JohnsonDave MoorcroftSteve OvettSolomon WarisoColin MackenzieDiane ModahlPaul EdwardsCliff Temple

Notice · page 5

Fred Denny, President of the R.W.A.

Over the years Belgravians have played a major role in the administration of Race Walking and now we congratulate Fred Denny upon his election as President of the Race Walking Association. Fred joined us in 1955 and regularly turned out for us (and against us as a member of the Metropolitan Police team) in 'mob' matches and 'opens.' His burly build made him a strong walker, developing a style which eliminated any wasteful and unnecessary movements. He acquired a good knowledge of style and technique which stood him in good stead when he retired from competition and became a judge. So, he became a familiar figure on the roadside, progressing to become a Grade I and Chief Judge. One of his priorities as President is to help and encourage younger walkers. We all wish Fred a happy and successful term of office.

Fred Denny

Notice · page 5

George Piddington ill

As we go to press we have sorry news to tell you of George Piddington. George is very ill indeed, in Alexandra Hospital, Portsmouth, after collapsing from a heart attack while out on a lunch-time run. Our long time Press Secretary, even though domiciled many miles away on Hayling Island, George has always been committed to his Club life. He has held a variety of posts in Belgrave, usually connected with our younger members and still has a fierce competitive streak left in him having several times competed for us last summer on Monday evening Veterans' T&F League matches (in spite of a round trip of 160 miles on the night). He still talks about that 'one more marathon' he wants to do. At his local Club Havant AC he is held in very high esteem. One only has to take a cursory glance through their Club Newsletter to see how involved he still is with athletics: organising teams, writing up results, judging at track meets, etc. His pride and joy are his Young Girls teams and just recently he has been writing excitedly to his old friends in Belgrave to lets us know how well they are coming on. Come on George. Pull through. We are all thinking of you.

George Piddington

Column · page 6

Sidetracks

○ Congratulations to Martina and Andy Ryan on the arrival of son Cathal on 1st October. Cathal weighed in at a massive 11lb 1½oz! We look forward to receiving him into the McDonald's League as a shot putter in the year 2007. Martina is looking well, running again and is already planning a cross country outing before the winter is over. On October 31st baby Rebecca Kulidzan made her debut, daughter of sprinter Barbara and husband Sasha. Congratulations again ... we look forward to seeing all three of you at the Women's Southern League come the summer.

○ Jackie Stone had a spell in hospital for a throat operation back in August but it's impossible to keep a Stone out of the action for long. Released from hospital on a Saturday morning, within two hours she was down at Crawley supporting the British League boys. We did notice that the old volume control had been turned down a bit though. Unfortunately 'Jacks' will be back in hospital for another op. at just about the time this magazine goes to press. We look forward to seeing you back in circulation again soon Jackie and in the meantime all your friends at the track and at Belgrave Hall will be thinking of you and wishing you well.

○ It was ironic that Bob Bridges, who had arranged for the Club to purchase instant "ice-packs" in case someone should pull a muscle, was to find that that the very first beneficiary was to be - himself! As Southern League Team Manager Bob still scores plenty of points for the Club by stepping in to any event where we find ourselves short. His favoured event is the hammer and down at Portsmouth it was amusing to see him throwing reasonably well only to come last while a single effort for a point in the pole vault resulted in an equal first place.

○ Veteran Alan Painter has been troubled by injury on and off for some time and his last race back on 15th January '94 resulted in the recurrence of a problem that not only put him out of contention for the Vets T&F League in the summer, but had him saying that he was giving up altogether! Nice to see him out for a jog on Christmas morning, however ...

○ Also on Christmas morning it was good to see two long time Belgravians turning up to watch the Lauriston Paarlauf. Ron and Tom Mitchell. Ron is coming up to 50 years of membership with the Club.

○ We've heard of some party games in our time - but musical pole vault! Back on March 28th at Ewell, Julia Cockram cleared a pb of 2.70 while young Michael Miller made it a Belgrave twosome by winning the U13 event.

○ When it came to the Guardian Gold Cup Final in September we were scratching around for steeplechasers and an unfit Matt Hillier was called into action. Unfortunately for Matt, one of our young men with really great potential, he chose that day to have an absolutely terrible run - a personal worst by minutes - and of course it was covered by Sky TV... and to complete his misery it just so happened to be broadcast when his mates were watching ... Happily Matt is now working his way back to form again and once more has qualified for a Surrey vest in the Inter-Counties CC - without running in the County Champs! Last year his train was held up; he missed the Youths' race but ran to an unofficial 4th in the Junior event. This year, in his first winter as a Junior, he didn't turn up at all, being ill in bed. Still got picked though - while Belgrave team mate Adam Jones who finished a worthy 9th was only rewarded with a reserve spot.

○ Budget cuts have forced the South of England AA to look closely at their administration and unfortunately this has resulted in Administrator John Jeffery being forced out. The "Southern's" loss is our gain, however, as John has found more time to help in the running of our Club. As a Senior Coach he has boosted the strength of our coaching set-up at Battersea this winter, he now occupies a place on our main committee, and has fitted very nicely into several of our current projects.

○ Veteran Tony Fairclough must be proud of the achievements of daughter Jo. A student in the USA at Iowa State University, she won their memorial 5k cross country race at Indian Creek in 17:51, having placed 7th in 17:38 at Minnesota a fortnight earlier over the same distance.

○ The British Athletics League has been taking a long look at itself in an attempt to solve the problems of gaining further publicity and avoiding the fixture clashes that often result in the top athletes missing league events. A mid-week super league has been discussed, along with changing the events in matches, changing the number of matches, holding them on Sundays and amending the time-table to coincide with results time on TV. No proposed amendments have found universal approval so far, however, and the set-up remains the same for Nineteen ninetyfive.

○ Here's an athletic trivia question that should get you guessing. Who was the leading Belgravian in the 1994 London Marathon as the field headed over Tower Bridge? Well, Gary Staines was doing the pace making at that point although also near the leaders were Paul Evans and Kassa Tadesse - must be Gary. Wrong. We didn't say anything about running! Swaying about on the back of the photographer's truck was Graham Adams, getting a grandstand view of his three team-mates as they mixed it with the world's best. Unfortunately all three of them came out over the next few miles leaving Jim Estall to take our Club Championship with a marvellous run for 70th place. If he can reproduce that form next year when he is 40 then he is going to surprise a lot of people.

○ The new Great Britain coaching line-up for 1995, unveiled in November '94, includes Belgravians Ray Hall, Race Walking, and Denis Costello, moving on from under-23 responsibilities to Long Jump. Stretching a point, we could claim an interest in Alan Lerwill, also coaching Long Jump. Alan was with us as a 2nd-claim member back in the late 60s, was the first Englishman over 26 feet (7.92m) and wasn't he the first (or one of the first) Briton over 7 feet in the high jump?

○ Rob Milner is collecting claims to fame. He's got two so far - sat on the toilet at Crystal Palace after Frankie Fredericks had used it and took Du'aine Ladejo's place in the Gold Cup final 4x400 relay.

○ "You will reach the top only through pain. The more troubles, the bigger the thrill of victory." So said Juha Vaatainen one of the 'greats' of Finnish distance running but the philosophy applies equally to all walks of our sport. Fraser Hazlett was all for giving up after rupturing an achilles tendon around the last turn of the 400m in the final British League match at Crawley. Incredibly he still managed to finish in a little over 50 seconds but what particularly disappointed him was the fact that it had been a long hard haul to get into the team and he had been running well. Would he ever get into a race of that standard again? But five days later he wandered down to Battersea track once more ...

Martina RyanAndy RyanCathal RyanRebecca KulidzanBarbara KulidzanSasha KulidzanJackie StoneBob BridgesAlan PainterRon MitchellTom MitchellJulia CockramMichael MillerMatt HillierAdam JonesJohn JefferyTony FaircloughJo FaircloughGary StainesPaul EvansKassa TadesseGraham AdamsJim EstallRay HallDenis CostelloAlan LerwillRob MilnerFrankie FredericksDu'aine LadejoJuha VaatainenFraser Hazlett

Feature · page 7

SUB-3 4 x 400 METRES ?

Now here's a challenge - a sub 3 minutes Belgrave 4x400 quartette. Our team would have to compete as part of an international field to be pulled out to their full potential but what a superb bonus it would be for our sponsors if we could pull it off - perhaps with TV coverage. Can someone, somewhere fix it for an all Belgrave squad to run against a British and/or other nations field in 1995?

Based on this year's rankings (and a conservative estimate for Mr. Regis) we could turn out a team for the 4 x 400 metres relay as follows:

But counting best or possible relay splits and remembering that NZ ex-400 metre Champion Callum Taylor has joined us (and he's been running low 47s in straight races):

Letter · page 7

Kassa's letter says it all

Kassa Tadesse

Fed up with turning out to judge events in the pouring rain? Just had a disagreement with someone on Committee because of differing views on how we should organise our affairs? Well, sometimes something will occur to make us realise why we do it, why it is all so very much worth while. A special award was made to Kassa Tadesse at the Club Dinner, to show how highly we regarded his Junior National Cross Country win. Our young hero responded with the following:

Respected Ladies and Gentlemen of Belgrave Harriers, This is the first time in my life I have been given an honour like this. Your appreciation of my efforts for the Club, and personally, is very moving. It lifts my morale and also increases my love of Belgrave Harriers and its members! But I haven't reached my goals yet. I have a lot more to do. For political reasons, I am here in London, and away from my country and my family. But I have found another family in the Harriers. Your support helps to make up for what I am missing. In return I promise to run more great races for Belgrave and encourage the rest of you, my friends, to victories too! Thanks to everyone for the honour you give me by this presentation. Good Luck! Kassa Tadesse

Kassa Tadesse

Notice · page 7

Membership Committee News: Nina is our new Welfare Officer

Ted Pallant is now heading up the Membership Management Committee. That is not to say that he is the person responsible for answering membership enquiries, recording of members' names, addresses and subscriptions, sending out fixture cards and magazines and all the one hundred and one other jobs that Carl Lawton has done for years - all that is still under the wing of Carl, our Membership Secretary. It's just that we now have a group meeting regularly to discuss and co-ordinate all matters to do with membership and Ted is the man who chairs those meetings. The group has already taken on board the responsibility for production of a small poster/brochure to be distributed to libraries, schools, tracks and anywhere else that might be a good place to advertise our facilities. The aim is to get our membership figures to top the 1,000 mark in the next eighteen months; currently we have around 650 on the books. Another task is the production and sale of Club kit. But one of the more innovative projects that has been started is the monitoring of the welfare of our members. All too often in the past we have lost track of those who have become ill and no longer able to keep in contact with the Club by attendance at our meetings. There have also been cases of those who have become long term injured and who would have appreciated the odd enquiry as to their progress and recovery. In the past this task has often fallen between the cracks in our overworked administrative set-up. Now we have someone whose specific task it is to keep an eye on this situation and keep in touch with those who would appreciate the occasional letter. Nina Mills has kindly volunteered to be our 'Welfare Officer' but she can only do a good job if she is kept informed of those who are having problems. So, if you know of anyone who is in poor health or would appreciate additional contact from the Club, then a 'phone call to Nina will set the wheels in motion. Nina Mills' address is [address withheld]. Telephone [phone number withheld]. She's waiting to hear from you.

Ted PallantCarl LawtonNina Mills

Luton, 15 Jan 94. "Always look on the bright side of life." In the Women's 6k race Gabby Collison slipped and fell, taking a little less interest in proceedings thereafter, while Matt Hillier would have placed somewhat higher in the Men's Under 17 event if he hadn't caught his shorts on the fencing and lost half a minute disentangling himself and wondering whether to go on without them! Feature · page 8

Colin Pearson, President 1994 ~ 95

Charlie Dickinson

by Charlie Dickinson

Nothing in life has come on a plate for Colin Pearson. Everything he has achieved has been by his own efforts. So to come up from the roots of the club to become President is no mean achievement; it is a position Colin holds with the utmost pride. Colin was born in 1943 in South London. When he was three his mother left home and his father, who had been injured in the war, was unable to look after him. As a result his Primary years were spent in a children's home in Croydon. At the age of eleven his father, living on a war pension, moved into a house in Earlsfield and rescued Colin from the home. He now attended Southfields Secondary school on the Merton Road and it was at this time that Colin first met Brian Gorman who later introduced him to Belgrave. It was at Southfields that he ran his first cross country race at the age of 13, winning in his age group and having his name announced in assembly. At 15 Colin started work as an apprentice printer in Baker Street at £2. 4s. 7d per week. Although not yet running, he got fitter at this time by cycling to work every day. He didn't complete his apprenticeship however, and at 18 joined the Army and was stationed at Aldershot. After five weeks the new recruits were given a fitness test; the standard was to run a mile in six minutes. Colin managed to keep up with the instructor and got nearer to five. Later he was instructed in heavy transport driving and became attached to the Royal Engineers at Bicester. He was also stationed in Cyprus as a UN soldier during the troubles there. It was in the army that Colin won his first running trophy. He ran the anchor leg for his regiment in a 4 x 440 yard relay beating the local staff for the first time ever. This was done off no serious training and in selecting the team, the instructor had just lined every one up and shouted go; the first four to finish were in the team. After three years in the army he was back in the print trade working for a firm in Wardour Street and living in Penwith road Earlsfield. Here he bumped into Brian Gorman again and was persuaded to join Belgrave. This was in the Spring of 1968 when Colin was 25 and that summer he joined the likes of Jim Rimmer, John Martin, Adrian Major, John Kelly, Philip Gee, the Hamilton twins, Phil Smith and Alan Mead in some of the Tuesday, Thursday and Sunday sessions at Battersea track, organised by Tom Carter. After all this training he had the shock of his life the following winter when he attempted cross country for the first time. All the others streaked away from him. It was Gerry North who quietly pointed out to him that the others were all training seven days a week, not just on club training nights. So Colin increased his mileage. By the early 1970's Colin had become a very good club athlete. The breakthrough came in 1971 when he ran 28:45 for the '5¾'. Bill Lucas praised him, congratulating him for breaking 30 minutes, assuming that he had run 29:45, and later was amazed to find that he had in fact run a full minute quicker. This performance put Colin into the 12-stage team ahead of Bill Kerr, much to the astonishment of Charlie Walker and the more established runners. In the following years he won two Southern 12-stage medals, ran in the National relay and won his County vest running in the Mitcham '15'. Colin never felt as happy on the track and took part more for fun. He did however run under 2 minutes for 800m, 4:05 for 1500m and found that he could get into the team by running the Steeplechase in which he got down to just over 10 minutes. Now driving for a living, it was at work that Colin had an accident jumping down off the back of a lorry which caused a very bad back injury that still plagues him. Although he tried several times to get back to running, the injury forced him to retire in 1980 and he began, instead, his wider involvement with the club. An active committee member for the last fifteen years, Colin first managed the Young Athletes' team, whose members at that time included: John Gladwin, Mark Sinclair, Mark Waller and Stuart Paton. Later he took over as Cross Country Team Manager from Gordon Biscoe, a position he held until last year. By now working nights on the newspapers, he used his Dormobile to ferry the team around and also helped organise club socials - and even cleaned out the changing rooms! Not surprisingly he was elected a life member of the Club. During the mid to late 'eighties Colin became heavily involved in the print unions. He was made deputy father of the chapel at Smith's in Wimbledon and was health and safety rep. for his firm. He was proud of and dedicated to his union work that involved hours of meetings. The hardest times were during the demonstrations at Wapping in the late 'eighties, events which changed the print trade and left Colin with bitter memories. His back problems became worse and eventually Colin retired from work through ill health in June 1991. Unfortunately, since then problems such as ME and other related viruses have affected his health - but luckily not his cheeky sense of fun nor his love for Belgrave. The highlights? So many memories; but among his best achievements as Road and Cross Country Manager was the outstanding Southern 12-stage victory in 1991. Colin had craftily kept his stars for the second half of the race. They ran five minutes quicker than the first six, storming through the field to surprise the pundits who had criticised his tactics before the race. He still has Justin Chaston's medal, given as a keepsake in recognition of his part in the victory. Then there was the time, back in the 'seventies, when as pack leader he pushed Jim Rimmer into Queensmere pond when he had dared to go to the front, disobeying Tom Carter's instructions. (Another one of Tom's tricks Colin learnt was to suddenly change direction when one of the youngsters was getting away.) Colin is proud of these memories and his involvement with Belgrave over the years. As President he would dearly love to see the distance running section improve and win the trophies they so often did when he was running in the 1970s. He is proud, too, to have his name as President on the board with those international distance stars from the past whom he has admired so much: Tom Carter, Bill Lucas and Arthur Penny. •••

Left: Another tigerish performance from Colin in a Club Championship 800 metres. He has twice won this title but on this occasion, in 1974, the verdict was given to Pete Carton (out of the picture on the right hand side. Both were given the same time. Photo by Ron Linstead.

Right: Colin (133) and Bill Laws (10) stride out together in the 1969 Walton '10'. Photographer unknown.

Feature · page 9

Club Rules a century ago

Preparing for the issue of a new Club Rule Book and examination of old books to compare the contents has brought to light some interesting items. The rules in force for the season 1895-96 included the following: 13—That any member violating the rules or guilty of misconduct, may be expelled from the Club by the votes of two-thirds of the members present at a special general meeting, which must be called for that purpose; and that any member absenting himself from the above meeting be fined 6d., unless a reasonable excuse be sent to the Secretary. 14—That the Club when out for a run shall be under control of a Pace-maker, such Pace-maker to be appointed by the Secretary or the senior member of the committee present. 15—That no member when out for a run shall be allowed to go a-head of the Pace-maker, unless the run be a racing one, or the signal having been given by the Pace-maker for a race home. In those far off days, just eight years after formation, the Club held meetings at their head-quarters, the "Kings Arms," Ranelagh Road, each Wednesday and Friday evening at 8.30 p.m. sharp. Club Colours were dark and light blue.

Obituary · page 10

A.D. 'Micky' Pyer (1903 - 1994)

CS

It is with sadness that we record the death of Micky Pyer. He was in his 73rd year of Belgrave membership - a record. Micky joined Belgrave on 21st October 1921: he was just eighteen years old and the First World War had finished only three years earlier. The shock of that War and its decimation of Britain's Youth had hit the country hard and it was well over a year before sports bodies were able to pick up the pieces and plan organised competition. The Area and National Cross Country Championships did not resume until 1920. Belgrave Harriers had survived the war, thanks to the sterling efforts of Bob Ricketts and Harry Hare, but it was not easy to build new teams. It was with much delight then that they received this new young member; and he wasted no time in showing them what he could do. Eyebrows were raised when he was our third scorer in a seven-club cross country race. Who was this new face? They were raised still further when he took the bronze medal in the Belgrave '7'! Jimmy Flatt joined the same day as Micky, and within eight months they had been joined by Bert Footer and Wally Bayliss. We now had a team that meant business, and when Ken O'Kell came along twelve months later it could safely be said that Belgrave had recovered from World War I. Micky never looked back; going on to win track and cross country Club titles and being a constant scorer for our championship teams. Eventually the claims of work took him away for a while but when he returned he took a particular interest in young athletes; qualifying as a AAA running coach and in later years as a AAA javelin coach. One of his protégés won the Surrey County Javelin title at both Junior and Senior level. For many years Micky was a familiar sight at Battersea Park, surrounded by youngsters of all ages. He was a kindly figure, regarded with much affection by all, and never had an unkind word for anyone. His loyalty to Belgrave was paramount and in recognition of his services he was elected President of Belgrave Harriers in 1971/72. Seventy-two years of membership is a very long time. Our Club has been fortunate indeed to enjoy his presence for so long; but we can be sure that his memory will remain with us for many years yet. Our sympathies are extended to his family. CS

April 1924 and A.D. 'Micky' Pyer proudly poses alongside the trophies he had amassed after less than three years of membership. Photo by Boyd's Studios, 34 Battersea Park Road.

A.D. 'Micky' PyerBob RickettsHarry HareJimmy FlattBert FooterWally BaylissKen O'KellCS

Obituary · page 10

Len Marchant (1921 - 1994)

Chas. Manning

We were very sorry to hear of the death Len Marchant, who died on October 26th after a period of illness. He had been a member since November 1936. Len was a bit of an outcast when I knew him in the '50s because he was in direct conflict with the A.A.A. He was aware of the damage that it would do to his amateur status but being a very good county standard runner he didn't see why he shouldn't capitalise on his talent. He took part in a couple of professional races over 880 yards but soon found that the rough tracks used for these events didn't allow him to get anywhere near his best times and, disillusioned, he tried to get back into amateur club running. Desperately he tried to get reinstated but the A.A.A. were adamant - even though he offered to pay back the few shillings he'd made. After a while, and by now living in Essex, he was allowed back into the sport - not to compete but to help set up the newly formed Basildon Athletic Club. Len regularly trained at Lauriston Cottage and I used to go with him. Ron Linstead was a particular friend of his (in fact Ron always used to tell me that Len would say, "Is that bloke Manning still with us?" and if the answer was, "Yes," then he'd put in a burst and drop me). Len always went to Arthur's davenport before a run to take the stopwatch. One Sunday (Len was always in control) we had been in the Park. Well, we'd had days of very heavy rain and the Brook was full. In those days the brick bridge wasn't there and we used to have to go through the Brook on all cross country runs at about that spot (200 yards west of the single track bridge). The regulars knew you could leap off the bank, three or four strides and up the other side. Normally we wouldn't cross it in training but on this occasion Arthur Whitehead said, as Len headed towards the footbridge, "Not going through the Brook then Len?" Len, feeling his leadership challenged, immediately struck out to the left and towards the brook. After the incessant rain the 'Beverley' was in full spate and was above the banks, so we didn't know where in the swirling, muddy waters the banks were. Faced with yards more water to cross than normal, we had to guess where to expect the big drop. I remember that I saw Len disappear completely except for one long arm, it was like Excalibur but with a stopwatch instead of a sword! Simultaneously I saw Steve Boreham sink entirely and as he came up (I didn't think he could swim) I grabbed him as he was being carried away and just dumped him clear of the water on the other side. Needless to say, we all survived. Our sympathy goes to Len's widow, Rose. Chas. Manning

Len MarchantChas. ManningRon LinsteadArthurArthur WhiteheadSteve BorehamRose Marchant

Report · page 11

Changes in line-up at AGM

The AGM in September saw a few alterations in our management line-up. Sue Porter had warned us nearly a year earlier that she would be standing down as General Hon. Secretary after a long stint as the main point of contact for all Club business. Sue is now helping to co-ordinate the deployment of our officials while Charlie Henn has stepped confidently into the breach. Our Hon. Treasurer Frank Ward has also retired back out of the limelight after a period looking after our finances that saw him make great savings for us while the job of managing the books gradually expanded to become the equivalent of running a small business. Frank has promised us that he will not fade completely into the background - perhaps he can concentrate on getting really fit again - and Derek Manning has now begun to ease himself into the hot seat. We thank both these very hard working people whose efforts on behalf of the Club have been invaluable. Two members were elected Vice-Presidents of Belgrave Harriers, both from entirely different sections of the Club. Pete Sutton has been a regular member of the Saturday crowd for longer than he would probably care to mention. He is one of those people who quietly gets on with helping out whenever and wherever he can. He still regularly has a jaunt over the Common but nowadays gets back early to "put the kettle on" for his customers. From the Coaching team Billy Bull has had a great deal to do with the effort we have been making to organise this side of our affairs - and like many of our coaches his care of our youngsters extends far beyond the onerous responsibility of making sure that he turns out in all weathers, several times a week, all year round; he is advisor, chauffeur and motivator too. The Committee have the scope to propose not more than two members for Life Membership in any year and on this occasion they came up with their full quota. The names of Frank Ward and John Regis, M.B.E. have now been duly added to the Life Members' Honours Board and we wholeheartedly congratulate them both.

Feature · page 11

Top Middlesex post for Glimpy

CS

Our congratulations go out to Maurice "Glimpy" Jones who has been elected President of Middlesex County AAA. He may be referred to as Maurice by most of his acquaintances these days but to Belgravians he has always been affectionately known as "Glimpy". Elected to Belgrave in 1948 as a 16 years old runner, he had already spent a couple of years mixing with athletes from both Belgrave and TVH; and it was something of a toss-up as to who he would join. His interest had been sparked by the "Kings of Speed" cigarette card series which featured Wooderson, Whitlock, Owens, etc.; and when he heard that Wooderson was to run at Tooting Bec in a ¾ mile record attempt he cycled there especially to witness one of his heroes. Travelling was in his blood and he became probably the most widely-travelled follower of major events. Since 1950 he has rarely missed an Olympic, European, Commonwealth or World Championship; and in 1952 he drove to Helsinki in an old car - quite an event in itself in those days. His own running did not reach the levels he was accustomed to watching but he ran regularly for our Junior team, finishing third in the L.C.C. Championships 880 yards one year. In 1961 he qualified as a coach at middle-distance and steeplechase, serving on the Belgrave Committee as both Coaching Hon. Sec. and Field Events Hon. Sec. Glimpy became our representative on the Middlesex Committee in the late 1970's and has served the County ever since. For seven years he was County Officials Secretary: a position he relinquished only when he became Middlesex County A.A.A. President for 1993/94. He follows in the footsteps of men like Alf Harley, Doug. Fotheringham and Ted Stimpson and we know he will maintain the standards they set.

CS

Report · page 11

Bels star on video

Did you catch the 10 minute film made by the all-Belgrave collaboration of Alistair Johnson and ex-International sprinter Ernie Obeng? It was shown on ITV on 12th January and seeing that it featured several of Ernie's poems it was aptly titled "Just Ten". The film conveyed the dedication and hard work applied by young people to achieve their athletic dreams but it was presented in an almost mystical way with the rhythm of the workouts being matched by the words and music. There were some great shots of Colin Jackson skimming the "Sticks In My Lane" but the boys and girls starting out on the hard road to success were all young Belgravians: Milton Holder, Syreeta Stracey, Marlon Dickson, Pam Friel and James Hilston. Watch out for another video shortie featuring Belgrave Harriers and filmed by one of the Cable TV companies. The sequence is nicely started by Clive Shippen talking about our Club's early beginnings while the camera zooms in on those old photographs taken outside the King William IV. Leo Coy then tells tales of today's British League team with the South of England AA Champs at Crystal Palace as the back-drop and the piece is concluded with a few minutes of dear old Tom Carter the link between the good old days and the good new ones.

Obituary · page 11

Joe Scamell

Doub

We are sorry to learn of the death in August, after a long illness, of Joe Scamell. He was seventy years old and joined us in 1944. No doubt he was encouraged by his father (also Joe) who walked for us between the two World Wars. Joe represented us for a number of years and enjoyed the seven and ten miles events on the road and regularly supported Club Handicaps on the track. Many will remember him as a quiet but enthusiastic walker, modest and always a pleasure to meet at an event. We extend our sympathies to his son Michael (still walking) and family. One lasting memory is that of Joe (senior), Joe, and Michael all competing in the same race - three generations of one family. The funeral took place at the City of London Crematorium, Manor Park. R.I.P.

'Doub'

Obituary · page 12

Tom Carter 1908-1994 An Appreciation

Clive Shippen

Has the ubiquitous Tom Carter really left us? Is it conceivable that he has departed the Club he loved so dearly? Was he not indestructible? Alas he was, and has gone - after 64 years of membership. His passing has left a void in this great Club of ours; an emptiness which will never quite be filled. No more shall we see that familiar ruddy faced, fair-haired figure at our meetings. No more that mischievous smile, that twinkling eye and that tugging arm as he conveyed with enthusiasm his latest race report - keeping the listener on tenterhooks to the very end so he could savour the result all the more. No more that wit and humour which brightened so many lives. All of these characteristics are irretrievably lost; but all are indelibly printed in our hearts and memories. How can it be otherwise when during his 64 years of membership he hardly ever missed a Club Dinner, Committee Meeting or Championship event?

It was as a novice that he joined Belgrave in 1930 and he immediately made his mark on the Club scene. With Bert Footer and Arthur Penny he formed the nucleus of our teams and in 1935 they took us to our first National Cross Country title. It says much for Tom's perseverance that he should repeat this feat 13 years later at the age of 40. We have not won a "National" since. Tom had a special rapport with young athletes. He spoke their language. For several years, in the sixties and seventies he coached them through to our Senior Championship teams. They are now veterans and they too remember him with much affection. His personality made him eminently suitable for organising our social events and for a time he was our Social Secretary. A rose or carnation invariably adorned his button hole, and under his influence the Club literally bubbled. In recognition of his service to Belgrave Harriers he was made our President in 1956 and again in 1983. It is a rare occurrence to be elected twice, and it illustrates just how much he was in the forefront of our affairs during that 27 years period. His second presidency at the age of 75 did not herald his retirement to the "back benches": far from it. His only retirement was from his own building firm, after which he spent even more time on Club affairs, devoting much of it to the well-being of Belgrave Hall. His generosity was legion and his home was an open house to Belgravians. Much Club business was chewed over at [address withheld] and much advice given. Tom seemed to know everyone and everything that was going on; probably because so many wanted to share their joys or sorrows with him. He was that kind of man: sympathetic, understanding and with a sense of humour that soon brought a smile to the most miserable of faces. Over 200 mourners attended his funeral at Thornton Heath on 4th January. They bade him farewell with the closing hymn "The Old Rugged Cross". In many respects Belgrave Harriers was Tom's old rugged cross; a cross to which he clung right to the end. We shall miss him dearly.

"So I'll cherish the old rugged cross Till my trophies at last I lay down: I will cling to the old rugged cross And exchange it someday for a crown"

To Kitty and Barry we offer our heartfelt sympathies.

Clive Shippen

Tom CarterBert FooterArthur PennyCharlie ManningKittyBarryClive Shippen
See it on the printed page →
Feature · page 13

Portrait Gallery No. 6—C. T. Carter

The following article, reproduced from Athletics (the monthly forerunner to Athletics Weekly), May 1946, shows that we were far from the only ones to appreciate Tom's sterling qualities

PORTRAIT GALLERY

No. 6—C. T. CARTER

The first experience Tom Carter had of running was in 1929, at the age of 21, when the local parson, who ran the football team in which Tom played, asked him to run in the 440 yards and mile in the London Federation of Boy's Clubs Sports at Chelsea. To everyone's surprise, including his own he won both events. The following year he ran second in the mile at the London Championships of the Junior Imperial League, and fourth in their National Championships when representing London. Tom very nearly joined Ashcombe AC, but, strangely enough, he liked the Belgrave vest and joined the 'Bels' in September 1930. Two months later he ran in his first real championship, the South of the Thames, and coming in 8th, was the first Belgrave man to finish. In his first Surrey county race he finished 12th, one of the winning team, and was a reserve for Surrey in the Inter-County event. He also ran in the Belgrave team in the Southern and National in his first season over the country. Ever since that year Tom Carter has been in Surrey's Inter-County team and in the Belgrave team for the County, Southern and National races. But it was not until 1936 that he really showed his best form. In that year he gained his first A.A.A. vest and England colours. He also ran third in the A.A.A. 10 Miles Championship when the late W.E. Eaton broke Shrubb's long standing record In the seventeen years he has been running he has won the Surrey 6 miles track championship, in a record time that still stands, Southern Counties 6 Miles, Inter Counties, British Workers National Championship (record time) and twice finished second in the Southern Steeplechase. Season 1940/41 was probably his best, with fourteen 'firsts' and one second in fifteen races. Few people realise the debt which Belgrave Harriers owe to Tom Carter, particularly during the war years when his infective enthusiasm and experience have been largely responsible for keeping the club to the forefront of British athletics. Always willing to help another athlete whether he be a novice, junior or senior, Tom is first and foremost a club man. Times beyond number he has led the 'Bels' to team victory, but he never hesitated to relegate himself to the 'B' team if, for any reason, he was not running well enough at the time. It was fitting when this year he won the Southern Cross-Country Championship, an honour which had previously eluded him. It is doubtful if there has ever been a more popular victory and, from the way he is running at the moment it looks as if there is plenty of life in the old dog yet. A real sportsman, and a popular one, it would seem strange to find the name of Carter missing from the sport down South.

C. T. (Tom) CarterW.E. EatonShrubb
See it on the printed page →
Item · page 13

Tom always managed to keep our feet on the ground and serious moments never lasted long with him nearby. Take the Belgrave Committee meeting before the 1994 A.G.M.: "Well I must say," said Bill Lucas, "that in 107 years of Club history I don't think there has ever been an occasion before when the books have not been audited in time for the A.G.M.!" Quick as a flash from the other side of the room came Tom's rejoinder: "Well then, that's a Club Record isn't it?"

Item · page 13

We thought at first he was Nurmi

Charlie Jones recalled 1930, the year when Tom joined the Club: "Tom Morell and I were down at Battersea track and we saw this slim blonde chap running round and looking really good. We thought at first it was the Finnish runner Nurmi. Neither of us had a membership form on us and when we went back to the track on later occasions he wasn't there. I didn't see him again all summer and heard a rumour that he had joined Surrey Athletic Club. Imagine my surprise then, when I turned up at the Club in the autumn for the Yacht '3' and saw him there - someone had got a form to him after all."

Charlie JonesTomTom MorellNurmi

Obituary · page 13

" ... it's a long straight old son " — Tom Carter 1908-1994 An Appreciation

Alan Mead

Next page ...

" ... it's a long straight old son "

TOM CARTER 1908-1994 AN APPRECIATION

I didn't know that Tom had been an international when I first met him but he still quickly became a hero. I was just one of a bunch of lads of similar age who seemed to become gathered together under his wing. He was the first adult I had come across who could chat to a youngster as a comrade; could laugh freely at the same jokes and run with an ease that totally belied his age. I was fourteen and he was fifty. Across the Common and into the Park we ran with Tom, a pack of young hounds cheerfully, and scarcely, kept in check by the old dog. We ran on Saturdays around Spankers Hill Wood or the Isabella Plantation, stopping off at the drinking fountain at Robin Hood Gate on the way back. We ran on summer evenings, Tuesdays and Thursdays, and never missed for any reason, even when the Common was ablaze and the fire engines had sucked the ponds dry; we ran along the Beverley Brook from bridge to bridge, eight or nine of us, splashing through the water and frightening the ducks before setting off again up the 'Toast Rack', legs streaked with mud and dust. Our mentor advised us on how to breathe while on the run ... length of stride ... the way to run up a hill and over the top. There were lung bursting relays around wooded, hilly circuits and sometimes, in the intervals between the sprints, we lay on our backs with legs supported by some old tree trunk as we let some of the 'tiredness' run back out of our lower limbs. At these times, with our backs cushioned by last year's bracken and with chests heaving, we gazed up through a green, lacy canopy to the blue sky beyond. This was a freedom never tasted before ... and rarely since. Even on winter nights we still ran on the Common, following the horse-rides, and when the mist masked out every glimmer of moon and stars we found our way by the feel of the path under our feet and the memory of daylight runs over the same route.

Four or five years passed in this way. Tom no longer found it easy to keep pace with his pack and used every trick in the book to ensure that we had to put in more effort than he did. He slipped more into the role of advisor from the trackside and took great pride in the fact that he had taken an ordinary bunch of South London boys and turned them into a team that was now winning Surrey and Southern Cross Country team medals and reaching a tantalising 4th place in the National. Individually one or two of his lads were now knocking at the door of international competition on the track and the country.

I was fourteen and he was fifty. Now I am fifty ... and he has gone. The intervening years saw him as coach, advisor, father figure, employer, 'matchmaker', ... true friend. It seemed a very special relationship, and it was; but the most amazing thing was that he was exactly the same with everyone he encountered. He was a unique man. A favourite piece of advice from way back, invariably given while one was stripping off for a quarter or half-mile race and accentuated by the way he gripped and pulled at one's elbow, was: "Now don't forget, it's a long straight old son". Incredibly, he delivered the same line in the same way just a couple of seasons ago before a veterans' race.

Well old chap, you've reached the end of your 'long straight' now, while we are just beginning to feel it's bite. But I'll bet my old spikes that when the time comes for us to cross that 'line', then your familiar old figure will be waiting just beyond the finishing post as it always has since we were young; waiting with some cheerful quip and ready to show us the way onward again. Until then, Tom, we'll miss you!

Alan Mead

Tom Carter
Born:18 May 1908
Died:22 December 1994
Joined Belgrave Harriers:4 September 1930
Belgrave President:1956-57 and 1983-84
First race:Yacht '3', October 1930, 4th fastest

Letter · page 15

Write on! — I shall always treasure his memory

P.W. "Jimmy" Green

Dear Editors, I learned with very much regret of the passing of Tom Carter. To me, Tom represented that great period of Belgrave history, when the London to Brighton Relay was "the" event of the year. In those days we were on opposite sides of the fence, before Surrey A.C. amalgamated with the Bels. There were many in Belgrave who knew Tom better than I, but from those early days there was an empathy which led to a life-long friendship. I came to know Tom well during the War years when we were both running for the Bel's two-miles team all over the country and which, I seem to remember, was never beaten throughout the War. The last time I spoke to Tom was on the 'phone when he was trying to find out more of the death and last days of my old RAF colleague Charlie Smart, a task which was to prove unsuccessful. Tom was a year older than me, but I shall always treasure his memory - a fine runner, a very good friend, and a great Belgravian. Sincerely, P.W. "Jimmy" Green

We are not absolutely sure but it seems likely that "Portrait Gallery", reproduced on page 13 from the magazine "Athletics" of 50 years ago, was almost certainly penned by "Jimmy" Green.

Tom CarterP.W. "Jimmy" GreenCharlie Smart

Letter · page 15

Chris running well

Chris Fairfield, Stalybridge, Cheshire

Dear Editors, Just a line to say that I have had my best season for 7 or 8 years, with 3:13:31 in the London Marathon after having my legs taken from behind by another runner at 15 miles and losing 20 minutes whilst having treatment, and two 1:20 half-marathons. I cannot puzzle why but seem to be getting better, winning 2nd places in both the Manchester YMCA 10k and the Hyde '7' in the over 55 age group on successive weeks. This coming after running both for over 20 years and never having a sniff of the prizes in all that time. Makes me feel there's still life in the old dog yet. Best of luck to all at the Club. Chris Fairfield, Stalybridge, Cheshire

Letter · page 15

Simply Centurions

Gordon Doubleday, Bath

Dear Editors, With respect one correction! You refer to the "Centurions AC." The name is simply Centurions. It dates back to 11th May 1911 when a group of walkers who had walked 100 miles inside 24 hours, in amateur competition, decided to form a fraternity of those who had accomplished this feat, to be called the Centurions. They are not affiliated to any organisation and do not promote events - although any 100m/24hrs walk will always have a few Centurions playing a large part on the organisational side. The recall of past Belgravians who suddenly make contact always interests me. I remember Jack Frost - a sprinter during World War II and Albert Frost - a regular member of our cross-country and road relay teams just after the War. Going back another decade I notice the name of Stan Scarsbrook amongst the new members. He must be the chap who in 1934 won the AAA 2m Steeplechase, followed by a win in the Empire (now Commonwealth) Games Steeplechase. Both events were held at the White City. - Another 1934 Empire Games winner was a certain A.W. Penny. Gordon Doubleday, Bath

Gordon DoubledayJack FrostAlbert FrostStan ScarsbrookA.W. Penny

Column · page 16

From The Belgravian — 50 years ago / 25 years ago

FROM THE BELGRAVIAN

50 YEARS AGO . . . . October 1944

"This edition might well be called the "Flying Bomb Number," as much of what is recorded took place whilst Southern England, and particularly London, was under attack by these missiles. In conjunction with Polytechnic Harriers, a full and ambitious programme had been arranged. A number of fixtures had to be cancelled, whilst the remainder were contested by scratch teams from either side. This was not surprising as Police and Civil Defence were fully occupied. A number of members were able to find or make the time to turn out for the Club, and we are all grateful to those who helped to keep Belgrave to the fore during most trying times.

A glance at the Club address book shows that we have many members living in some of the most heavily damaged areas. No serious injuries to members have been reported, but many of our members' homes have been damaged, some completely destroyed. To all who have suffered the Club offers their deepest sympathy, and we hope that lost and damaged homes will be quickly restored. We also extend condolences to those who have lost relatives and friends."

25 YEARS AGO . . . . December 1969

"The 1969 track season was extremely disappointing and was climaxed by our relegation from the third division of the National League to the first division of the Southern League. We only managed to annexe one trophy, The Jim Peters Trophy, which we only won with the considerable assistance of a strong contingent of juniors and youths."

"I am sure that everybody knows that Belgrave are relegated from the National League. This time last year we were considering which club would be promoted with us into the second division, but when it came to it we fizzed out like a damp firework." (Field Event Captain).

"During the RWA year 1968-69 Belgrave Harriers' Walking Section equalled, if not surpassed, the achievements of the great club teams of 1957 and 1960. The year ended with the club having won many races including the classic London to Brighton and three of the four National Senior Road Walking Championships. The walkers also secured second place in the remaining Senior event and a fourth place in the National Junior 5 miles. In 1957 and 1960 when Belgrave teams won all three Senior titles (a fourth, the National 20 Kilos, was added in 1965), the club had the advantage of being able to call upon the services of such internationals as Stan Vickers, Eric Hall and George Williams. Today, with only one international, Ray Middleton, among the active club walkers, success depends more on the efforts of the ordinary clubman."

"Ray Middleton finished 5th in the European Championships 50Km Walk for the second time. After nearly 4½ hours of effort he crossed the line only 4 minutes away from a bronze medal. He already owns a Commonwealth Silver Medal."

Stan VickersEric HallGeorge WilliamsRay Middleton

Report · page 16

British M50 record for Snowy

Snowy Brooks won the BVAF Decathlon Champs. at Sheffield on 24/25 September by nigh on 400 points from Angus of the USA with the first Briton 600 points behind in 4th. His 6736 was an M50 British Record even though he felt that none of his individual performances was outstanding and that a couple, by his standards, were quite poor. It was also his first competition with the lighter M50 implements and Snowy feels he did not achieve all that he might have with them, being unused to the weights. Training partner Nick Phipps took the M40 event with our second-claim member Pan Zeniou 5th, 5176. Having achieved this year's target, Snowy is now aiming for the World Championships in Buffalo, USA, in 1995 and he must have a good chance of a high placing, for Angus, well behind him here in Sheffield, has been the US M50 Champ for the last two years.

Feature · page 16

A new racing machine for Matthew Coleman

Ted Pallant

As you will know, Matthew is a wheelchair competitor for Belgrave and this year I've had the pleasure of meeting him several times at the Guardian Junior Wheelchair Grand Prix events. It's wonderful to see him determined to perform at his best in the colours of our club. Born in Newport Pagnell on 11th November 1977, Matthew, although handicapped, was determined to take part in sport, his first interest being basketball, followed by wheelchair short-court tennis. In 1992 he became involved in wheelchair racing, joining the British Wheelchair Racing Association and the British Sports Association for the Disabled. During 1993 he qualified to compete for East Anglia, winning the 100, 200, 400, 800 and 1500 metres in the trials, and represented the Area in the B.S.A.D. 1993 National Champs. in which he won the 400 and 800 metres. Having recently completed his G.C.S.E.s and passing the "City & Guilds" in Business Studies, he is now at college in Huntingdon, taking the B.Tech National Computer Studies course. Other interests in his life are music - and occasionally he can be found alongside the river, fishing. And lastly, after a very successful season, at the Guardian Gold Cup Final in Bedford, our Patron Sir Eddie Kulukundis, OBE., who has taken a great interest in Matthew's sporting career, had pleasure in presenting a very excited young man with a new racing wheelchair that he had kindly donated.

Ted Pallant

Matthew ColemanSir Eddie KulukundisTed Pallant

Report · page 17

"Southern" centenary — South of England CC Champs. Parliament Hill, 29 Jan 94

The "Southern" celebrated one hundred years of existence in 1994 and it was also 40 years ago that the race was first held at Parliament Hill. It seemed fitting, then, that such a landmark should be celebrated with something a little different and for the first time the men's and women's events came together to make a very full afternoon's racing. Unfortunately Belgrave could not add much to the event in the way of team competition, being unable to complete a squad in any race except the U17M and U15B categories and even there high hopes were devastated by the 'flu' bug which knocked several of our leading youngsters out before the start. The last Belgrave runner to win the Southern must have been Gerry North in 1965 - so long ago that Dave Bedford was a mere Youth (as was our own Chas Dickinson running for TVH in those days). That year the mud on Parliament Hill was far worse, ankle deep at the bottom along to the finish by the running track. Nowadays the drainage seems improved in spite of the winter rains; the going was soft and the course tough, but perhaps better than expected. The Women's race over 6.5 kilometres saw Gabby Collison making an impression among the best cross country runners that the South can offer. Her very creditable 16th was followed up by good runs from Martina Ryan and Jacinta Moore ... but there it stopped. One more runner and it is highly likely that we would have placed 4th or 5th in the team race. It was Belgrave star Kassa Tadesse who stirred the memories of the faithful Belgrave supporters with a run-away win in the Junior race. After biding his time for the first mile or so, Kassa seemed to change gear at the back of the course to surge clear of the talented young Blackheath runner Mark Steinle. By the three mile mark he was 70 yards up and he forged on to finish nearly a minute clear. Although Kassa hates the English mud, he was simply in a different class from the rest. In the Senior race Jim Estall had a fine run finishing in 44th position. After a long period of injury last summer, Jim has worked hard to improve his running style. He has cut back on his usual heavy racing schedule, particularly on the roads, and the Autumn saw him race sensibly at about 90% effort. Jim looked very relaxed throughout his run and intended to follow up with a PB in the London Marathon. Elsewhere among the Seniors there were steady runs from Owain Lewes, Mike Webb and Mike Lake (running for Alton), but Belgrave were unable to field a scoring six and a half fit Tony Stone turned out as fifth scorer. Senior Men 1, J.Downes (London Irish) 44:53; 2, A.Bristow (Brighton & Hove) 45:02; 3, D.Bannister (Shaftesbury Barnet) 45:59; ... 44, J.Estall 49:28; 193, O.Lewes 52:49; 195, M.Lake (Alton) 52:49; 209, M.Webb; D.Anderson and A.Stone also finished; 494 finished. Report · page 17

Kassa - our first ever "National" winner — English CC Champs North Shields, 12 Mar

A very small Belgrave presence at North Shields was treated to a historic occasion as Kassa Tadesse became the first Belgrave Harrier ever to win an English National Cross Country title. Kassa has been troubled by a persistant knee injury and in the week leading up to the event it seemed pretty doubtful whether he would compete. However, an easy few days followed by a trial run beforehand showed that the problem wasn't getting any worse and the decision was taken: the nation's under 20s were to find the race that much tougher as a result. The Belgrave runner was handily placed as the race got away and by the end of the first lap, together with Bruno Witchalls of Mole Valley and the City of Stoke runner Ben Sutton, a gap was beginning to open up before other pretenders to the title came through. Bruno had already met defeat by Kassa in the Surrey League and County Championships but the Mole Valley man showed no sign of an inferiority complex, attempting to make a telling effort even while the Belgravian was pushing the pace. It was too much for Sutton who dropped back a little although holding his place ahead of the pack. But as the second lap unwound it was now Witchalls under pressure. A ten metre gap became twenty ... and then thirty ... and Kassa was away ... to achieve something that no Belgravian has ever done before. Senior: 1, D.Lewis (Rossendale) 42:35; 2, J.Nuttall (Preston) 42:51; 3, J.Downes (London I) 43:18; ... 391, M.Lake (Alton) 50:02; 375, M.Webb 49:54; 1550 finished. Teams: 1, Blackheath 171; 2, Bingley 203; 3, Morpeth 214; ... 165 teams closed in but Bels didn't. U20M 1, K.Tadesse 29:54; 2, B.Witchalls (Mole Valley) 30:07; 3, B.Sutton (City of Stoke) 30:11; ... 131 M.Anderson 35:19; 186 finished. Teams: 1, Coventry God. 68; 2, London Irish 116; 3, Wirrall 199; ... 15 teams closed in but Bels didn't. Results · page 18

Belgrave T&F Championships 1994 - Battersea Pk

Saturday 10 July

For the first time in quite a few years we reverted to the format of holding all our main championship events on a single afternoon. Comment was almost unanimously favourable and it is expected that we will do the same again in 1995. The weather was beautiful and the atmosphere was rather like a huge family picnic. 'Burgers were on sale and there was a plentiful supply of drinks (alcoholic and otherwise) for our hard working officials - and when it came to stock taking at the end of the day the meeting organiser was astounded to find out just how many officials there must have been! As to the actual athletics: 99 Belgravians took part, entering an average 2.3 events each - 33 women and 66 men. The sprints fared particularly well with no less than 10 taking part in the senior men's 100 metres but it has to be said that the great majority of competitors were either under 20 or over 40. Wednesday 20 July

Club 5,000m Championship for the Griffen Cup.

A strange race this one. Phil Carstairs took the lead from the gun to set the gentlest of paces and yet the field immediately became strung out with Roger Alsop 30 metres in arrears. After four laps the field concertina'd again and with six to go Roger burst ahead aggressively to take a 40 metre lead in one lap and then stretch it to 100 metres in two. With Phil now flagging, Said Chettati and Marcello Bizio were left to alternate in 2nd and 3rd spots until the latter ran across the line clearly in 2nd place. Said was delighted with his first ever Belgrave medal - it will not be his last - but less happy was young Rob Draper who had arrived in time for a 7 o'clock start and had to bitterly watch the medals go to his rivals who had been sent on their way at half past six: he had to settle for a 9:20 run as a guest of VAC in the 3k. 1, R.Alsop 15:20.8; 2, M.Bizio 15:51.6; 3, S.Chettati 15:55.8; 4, P.Carstairs 16:13.4; 5, D.Williams (gst) 17:05.4; 6, M.Manning 18:27.9; 7, R.Bale 19:37.9.

Wednesday 7 September

Club 10,000m Championship for the Tom Carter Trophy.

The pleasure of circuiting the Battersea oval 25 times (26 insisted one aged competitor) on a cold evening in September was shared with Veterans AC as we combined our 10k track championship with theirs. It did at least make for some exciting lap scoring! Some nine sets of scorers, each checking off the laps of four runners should have made for an easy job but with athletes getting lapped time and time again, and with the light failing ... All problems were settled in the end and there never was any doubt about the Belgrave competitors who headed the field. Roger Alsop was well in control and added the Tom Carter Trophy to his collection with ease, throwing in a 65 second last lap as a final flourish in this fastest run in our championship for no less than 19 years! And whose time was it back in'75, still standing up well against today's? Why, none other than the man placing 7th in the latest edition of the event. Laurie O'Hara is still knocking out fast times and on this occasion he removed no less than 40 seconds from Bob Belmore's VAC M60 record set in 1988. 10000m: 1, R.Alsop 31:43.3; 2, P.Carstairs 32:30.1; 3, S.Chettati 33:06.2; 4, M.Webb 33:18.1; 5, L.Lyons 34:38.3; 6, A.Armstrong 35:50.8; 7, L.O'Hara 36:21.4; 8, Marlon Anderson 37:33.6; 8, T.Williams 38:32.7.

Report · page 19

Du'aine Ladejo - European Indoor Champion — Paris, 11-13 March

Du'aine Ladejo - European 400m Champion. Has a nice ring to it doesn't it, especially with the more prestigious outdoor event coming up later in the year. A very comfortable 47.33 saw Du'aine through round one with 3rd place in his heat but it was in the semis where Europe had a first glimpse of the Briton's potential when he unleashed a 46.26 to win and make sure of a berth in the final. As it happened, that sort of pace could not be matched by any of the opposition and a 46.53 clocking carried him to the "gold". Paul Edwards came oh! so close to qualifying for the shot putt final. A place in the first 12 or 18.80 was the target and Paul found it just beyond him as he reached 18.49 and 14th. Two hundred metre man Phil Goedluck also failed to go beyond the first round, in spite of nearing his best ever with 21.31.

Du'aine LadejoPaul EdwardsPhil Goedluck
