Feature · page 61

Athletics in 1950

Arthur Bruce

Judging by the letters and comments we receive, Arthur Bruce's reminiscinces of athletics after World War II are among the most popular items in our magazine so we have pleasure in continuing with:

ATHLETICS IN 1950 by Arthur Bruce

The year of 1950 was one of transition for Great Britain. The rationing era was ending and the improvisation of the immediate post-war years was ending. More important, the generation of pre-war stars that had carried on after the war such as Sydney Wooderson and Don Finlay were now being supplanted by a new generation of athletes, some of whom were only at the beginning of high calibre careers. The climax to the year for British athletes would be the British Empire Games in New Zealand in February followed by the European Games at the Heysel Stadium, Brussels, in August. Both were to show the steady improvement of performances throughout the athletic world.

The winter season preceding the Empire Games produced several new young cross country performers coupled with those making their mark from previous winters. The first major event of the year was the Inter-Counties Championship held at Leicester and it produced a clear win for Dr. Frank Aaron the Yorkshire Champion who pulled clear of the young prodigy Geoff Saunders who only four years previously had won the first National Youths' Championship. They were followed in by the T.V.H. star Alec Olney and another young rising luminary Walter Hesketh from Manchester A.C. Lancashire were clear in the team championship with a fifty points margin over Warwickshire.

The Inter-Counties were followed in February by the district championships, the Southern at Eastbourne surprisingly won by Harry Hicks (Hampstead) in front of more experienced performers with T.V.H. taking the team honours in front of Aylesford Paper Mills, Blackheath and Belgrave. An interesting newcomer to the Juniors was Gordon Pirie, over a minute in front of his rivals, including brother Peter, South London H also taking the team honours.

The Midland at Great Barr the same day went to the veteran performer Bobby Reid of Birchfield by only a second from team mate Ted Dalton with their club comfortably taking the team title. Inter-County Champion Aaron was the victor of the Northern at Horwich from Geoff Saunders and Don Birch of Leeds with the quality small club Sutton Harriers clear in the team event.

The following month at Aylesbury the National Championships were resolved. The "Senior" produced another convincing win by Frank Aaron from Alec Olney, Reg. Gosney and Harry Hicks, with the team title going again to the excellent packing of the Northern Champions Sutton Harriers whose first five scorers were home in twentyfive places! Second were Harehill Liberals and then the Scottish Champions Shettleston Harriers. The "Junior" went to the powerful running of Walter Hesketh from Green of Reading and Jack Eastham of Bolton, team title going to East Cheshire Harriers. Youth winner was Ike Williams, clear of his rivals with Sutton Harriers taking the team title.

Our team for the international at Brussels at the end of March, stronger and younger than immediate post-war years, was not fast enough to cope with the track oriented French and Belgian teams spearheaded by Alain Mimoun and Lucien Theys. Although Aaron and Olney ran well for 4th and 7th places respectively, the winning time of 45 minutes told it all - a different conception of cross-country running.

The Empire Games

The winter season was bisected by the timing of the Empire Games which took place in Eden Park, Auckland, New Zealand, in the first week of February. Despite competing out of season against the in season Australian and New Zealand performers the Home Countries did well with fines wins by John Parlett in the 880 yds, Len Eyre in the 3 miles, Dorothy Tyler in the high jump, Tim Anderson in the pole vault and Jack Holden in the marathon. Other sterling performances were those of Les Lewis, 2nd in the 440 yds, Alan Chivers, 2nd in the 6 miles, and Dr. Moody in the shot. Our teams took runners up spot in both relays. Our women's team, however, suffered from the very high level of performances set by the Australians and New Zealanders.

The track season begins

The track season was preceded by the usual short road relay period culminating in April with the London to Brighton Relay. A closely fought battle in the shortened version of the race, starting at Mitcham, resulted in a win by T.V.H. from Blackheath and Reading. The opening shots of the track season were fired at the White City on 18th March being the Inter-Varsity match between Oxford and Cambridge. The outstanding performer was Roger Bannister in the mile in 4:14.8, a new Inter-Varsity record and an indication of better things to come. Other names to go on to better things were John Wilkinson and J.M.K Finley in the sprints, Angus Scott in the 880 and Chris Brasher in the 3 miles.

The first major meeting of the year at which the shape and strength of British Athletics would be expressed was the annual Inter-Counties Championship coupled with selected invitation international events; these were held at the White City over the Whitsun weekend. The first day again showed E. McDonald Bailey to be our outstanding sprinter, winning the invitation 200 metres in 21.3 by five metres and close to the British record. The other international event, the 800, was won by the massive Canadian performer Bob McFarlane. The domestic events resulted in good wins by Harry Whittle in the 440 hurdles, Alec Olney in the 3 miles and a new British 7 miles walk record in 51:21 by Lol Allen of Sheffield by four minutes. The second day's programme produced a record equalling run in the domestic 220 by McDonald Bailey in 21.2, a good 440 yds win by Olympic Champion Arthur Wint in 48.1 and the first of many wins by John Disley in the 2 mile steeplechase.

Another prominent early season meeting, the Kinnaird Trophy, which some regarded as the English Club Championship meeting and was promoted by Polytechnic Harriers in conjunction with the annual Poly Marathon from Windsor to Chiswick, provided good early season competition and a British record equalling long jump of 25' 2¾" by S.Williams of the Polytechnic. Jack Holden won the marathon in confident style by over five minutes from A.Lawton of Leeds Harehill. Polytechnic Harriers took the team honours of their own promotion.

A.A.A. Championships

The A.A.A. Championships followed in July being the selection meeting for the forthcoming European Games in Brussels. The Friday night's events provided a usual hors d'oeuvre for the main events on the Saturday, with a sparkling 6 miles in which Frank Aaron fought an exciting duel with Van de Wattyne the Belgian Champion, finally drawing away for a new British Record of 29:33.6 with Mihalic of Yugoslavia 3rd. The other prominent final on Friday was Duncan McClarke's new British hammer record of 178'4½" from Gubijan of Yugoslavia.

Saturday started with a British record in the 7 miles walk of 50:11.6 by Roland Hardy of Sheffield closely followed by his team mate Lol Allen. The half mile produced the expected battle between Arthur Wint the Olympic 400 metre Champion and Roger Bannister the future World mile record holder with John Parlett, the Empire Games 880 yds Champion thrown in for luck! Wint came home in 1:51.6 to equal the Championship Record from Bannister and Parlett. The field events continued to slowly improve with a close high jump battle between Alan Paterson and Ron Pavitt, victory going to the former with a record equalling leap. The 440 yds produced its usual tight finish with Les Lewis getting home from Derek Pugh in a record equalling 48.2. The mile, which was considered to be very open, showed a return to form of the holder Bill Nankeville who won in 4:12 from Empire Games Champion Len Eyre and Alan Parker. The 3 miles provided another great battle with Lucien Theys of Belgium, a popular visitor here, beating Alec Olney and a fast finishing Andy Ferguson in 14:09. Another title going overseas went to Segedin of Yugoslavia in the 2 mile steeplechase although John Disley, in finishing a close 2nd, indicated a future Olympic medal winner's potential. McDonald Bailey collected his usual double in the sprints to maintain his control of the British sprint scene. The final event, the 440 yds hurdles produced a class battle between Harry Whittle and Angus Scott and a win for the former but providing us with two good representatives at Brussels.

Climax of the season

And so to the climax of the season for British Athletics: The European Games at Stade Heysel in Brussels in late August. In the first final at which good results were expected, both British hopes Lol Allen and Roland Hardy were disqualified after finishing 2nd and 3rd respectively in the 10kms track walk. The less said about the standard of judging the better! Our lesser hopes in the 50kms walk were confirmed although Alf Cotton and John Proctor walked well to finish 5th and 6th.

The superiority of the Russian women in the power events was early established by Nina Doumbadze in the women's discus, winning by some six metres from her nearest rival. However, British hopes were revived by Sheila Alexander and Dorothy Tyler in the high jump where they placed 1st and 2nd with our third string Betty Crowther finishing 5th.

Our men swung into action in a nail-biting 800 metres in which Roger Bannister and Marcel Hansenne swept past new Norwegian discovery Audun Boysen only to have a flying John Parlett take the title right on the line in 1:50.5. Meanwhile Maureen Dyson, refighting her 1948 Olympic battle with Fanny Blankers-Koen, again collected the silver medal in the 80 metres hurdles, although the margin was greater.

Zatopek!

Zatopek's 10,000 metre win on the first day was significant in that on a 400 metre track he would have lapped the whole field, showing his tremendous superiority at this distance. The 5,000 metres final was eagerly awaited by the home crowd supporting their hero Gaston Reiff, the Olympic Champion renewing his Olympic battle with Emil Zatopek. The race took on a predictable course with the elegant Reiff stretching Zatopek on the rack for eleven laps only to have him race past in irresistable style with only a lap to go, a dismayed Reiff fading to be caught on the line for second place by a fast finishing Mimoun of France. Alex Olney ran his usual solid race for 8th place in 14:51.

Our fortunes in the sprints were mixed. Despite looking a finallist in the 100 metres, won by Bally of France, Brian Shenton, showing the best form of his life, repeated his 21.5 best performance in semis and final for a totally unexpected win in the 200 metres. This was followed by the ever-consistent Alan Paterson taking the high jump in 1.96, one place better than his 2nd in Oslo in 1946. Another fighting performance from Harry Whittle gaining bronze in a good quality 400 metres hurdles final made you wonder what he would do if he could only hurdle!

Fanny Blankers-Koen kept up her winning ways in the 200 metres, drawing away from Setchenov of the Soviet Union with Dorothy Hall taking third spot. The metric mile provided another storming finish with Bill Nankeville breaking clear of the field with 400 to go only to be collared close to home by Willi Slijkhuis of Holland and El Mabrouk of France in 3:47.2, the favourite Lennart Strand of Sweden not even finishing.

The steeplechase produced another Czech in the Zatopek mould. Jan Roudny pulled clear of all the expected favourites in 9:05.4 including our own John Disley who finished 10th, having an uncommon off day.

The relays again produced mixed fortunes for us as Britain did not make the first three in the sprint relay but were led by our new European Champion Derek Pugh in a tight finish with the Italians. A surprise win by our women's relay team after some sloppy baton changing by the Dutch and Soviet teams gave us an unexpected victory.

Comparing our overall performances with the previous European Games indicated the progress made in our track and field standards, especially regaining our power in middle distance running, but our weaker events remained as before on the field.

And what of Belgrave?

And so we come to Belgrave's position in the span of British Athletics in 1950. The athletic scene over 40 years ago, our objectives and standards, were so different from what we observe today. No women's section, no British League, no Grand Prix Series and only a small percentage of the road races that we enjoy today.

However, athletics in 1950 did have a structure which enabled athletes to fasten on to their priorities. For Belgrave 1950 was also a year of transition. Our strength, especially over the country, immediately after the War was the result of the quality performers from pre-war that were still available or inherited when athletics recommenced in 1945. By the year 1950 most of these stars were at the end of their careers and the junior talent that had joined immediately after the war had not yet come through. The cross country season indicated the situation as to this decline first in the County Championships. Belgrave were 2nd in the Surrey Champs. both in the team and individual, Len Herbert following Stone of Dorking St. Pauls and with only two in the first ten. Our performers in Middlesex under the name of Cadogan AC slipped to 4th team, only Derek Burfitt, 9th, and Vic Blowfield, 10th, making the county team. We did, however, have Jack Brown in 6th place in the Kent Championship. This situation was confirmed a few weeks later in the Inter-Counties, our first scorer in 52nd place being Bill Lucas - Surrey's third man. The Belgrave 10 mile Cross Country Championship at this time resulted in a win for Jack Brown in 59:31 from Steve Charlton and Bill Lucas.

The Southern Champs the following month at Eastbourne at which the Club usually made a strong showing resulted in another 4th place behind T.V.H., Aylesford and Blackheath, our first man home being Les Stokell with a good performance by evergreen Ted Stimpson in 21st place. Our Juniors were a solid 5th with Charlie Walker 24th while our Youths were runners up with Don Maclean a good 4th in the individual.

The National Cross Country Champs in March only reflected for Belgrave their previous performances. Eighth place would be considered today a good performance, especially with the scoring six in 100 places. Les Stokell was first home in 47th place. Sutton Harriers again proved their solidarity with their scoring six in 67 places. The Juniors were back in 21st with a solid run from Charlie Walker in 40th place and the Youths performed creditably in 7th spot with Don Maclean in 20th position.

Meanwhile our walkers put up a good performance in the RWA 10 mile Championship, being 2nd to Sheffield with ever-consistant Harry Curcher second individual to Lol Allen and a good follow up performance by Charlie Churcher in 5th place.

It's interesting to see the result of the '5¾' Road Championship that year with a win by Vic Blowfield in 29:09 from Bill Lucas and Charlie Walker.

The Southern Clubs' London to Brighton Relay enabled Belgrave to display their strength in depth with a close up 4th and good runs by Derek Burfitt, Charlie Walker, Len Herbert and Bill Lucas.

Our walkers continued their string of good performances with a close 2nd in the National 20 mile Championship to Woodford Green with Harry Clayton in 4th spot.

The track season started annually with the County Champs. In the Surrey Bill Lucas won the 3 miles with 3rd places in the mile by Charlie Walker, the steeplechase by Eddie Short and in both sprints by Bas. Walden. Our only notable performances in the subsequent Inter-County Championships at the White City was a good win by Harry Churcher in the 2 mile walk and a fighting 2nd in the 3 miles by Len Herbert.

Our performers in the other county champs. did well with Derek Burfitt and Vic Blowfield 1st and 3rd in the Middlesex 1 mile and Jack Brown 3rd in the Kent senior mile.

In the RWA 50k Championship Belgrave maintained their good record in walking champs with 2nd team to Woodford Green, Clayton again walking well to take 3rd place.

And so to the AAA Championships at the White City in July with a notable performance by veteran Charlie Smart in the 6 miles, finishing 5th overall but 3rd Englishman home! This was matched by Readman's 5th place in the 7 mile track walk behind Roland Hardy's British record. Our only other performance of note on the main day was from Bill Lucas, who always timed his best performances for championships. He finished a close 4th in the 3 miles, only 2 seconds behind winner Lucien Theys of Belgium, timed at 14:09. Bill subsequently represented Britain in the triangular meeting against USA and Turkey at the White City.

"Athletics Weekly" first made its appearance in 1950, before that year being only a monthly publication. Editor was P.W. "Jimmy" Green - now a Life Member of Belgrave Harriers. The cover picture for March 25th 1950 shows Dr. Frank Aaron leading the Senior National Cross Country Championship at Aylesbury after 2 miles. Others in the picture are Hicks, behind Aaron, Olney (811), Birch (343) and Eyre (349).

The International Cross-Country Race 1950 style. Lucien Theys of Belgium (centre) was the eventual winner and Alain Mimoun of France (right) was 2nd. An estimated 20,000 spectators turned up to watch the race, held in Brussels. Photo from World Sports.

Wimbledon Common c.1950, with Len Coleman (far left) and Harry Churcher (second from right) race walking four abreast along The Causeway with not the slightest need to worry about the traffic. Photographer unknown.

Does anything change?

"Belgrave Harriers started the winter season well by winning the Chelmsford Road Relay last Saturday. Will they regain their former position in cross-country circles this season? Much may depend on the willingness of some of their track stars to take their winter sport seriously . . . "

Athletics Weekly, November 11th 1950

Above: Twenty two year old Haroun Korjie, second only to John Regis over 100 metres in 1994.

Right: Gavin Mason pauses between reps while Clive Shippen gets busy with the camera. When Gavin clocked 48.89 to place 7th in the British League 'A' 400 metres on 2nd July he further complicated a little statistical anomaly. John Gladwin's Under 20 Club Record set in 1989 is a hand timed 48.9, but going back to 1977 Dave Mote also ran 48.9 as a Junior for Surrey AC. Surrey amalgamated with Belgrave in 1982 and therefore Dave had a 50% (and little known) claim on that record.

Left: New under 17 Club Record holder at 56.39 and 10th best in the country for his age, Rob Milner also competed regularly over the 91.4cm barriers and set a Club Best for a 17 year-old of 58.3. Photo by Robin Nicholls

Above: Aussie Mike "Mad Dog" Morgan was hoping to make the Commonwealth Games team for Wales in the Long Jump. Unfortunately he found out that they insist that their athletes must have competed in the Welsh Championships for at least two years. "It's bloody embarassing," he said, "I might have to consider becoming a flamin' Pom!

Report · page 73

Kassa beats huge field over 17.5k

Bill Laws

Kassa Tadesse produced further confirmation of his steady improvement as a road racer when he won the inaugural 17.5k Pont de Normandie Classic on 21st January, writes Bill Laws. Over 10,000 lined up as part of the celebrations to mark the opening of what is now the world's longest bridge spanning the Seine estuary at Le Havre. A six hour storm-swept ferry crossing did not deter the tough 21 year old from defeating a strong select field competing in a race held in high winds and driving rain. The bad weather ensured a steady start. After 4k a small group of five broke away with Kassa tucked in. At the 8k point Kassa surged up a sharp incline and a gap appeared which he gradually extended until by the finish he was over a minute clear of former French half marathon champion Didier Bernard. 1, 1, K.Tadesse 51:56; 2, D.Bernard 53:01.

Kassa TadesseBill LawsDidier Bernard
See it on the printed page →
Obituary · page 73

Tragic death of Michael Coleman

Michael Coleman, who over the last few years has been mentor and manager to Kassa Tadesse, tragically collapsed and died while jogging. By a cruel twist of fate his untimely death occurred in Brockwell Park just hours after Kassa had won the Pont de Normandie Classic and Michael was never to receive the news which would have given him so much pleasure.

Michael ColemanKassa Tadesse

Records · page 74

1994 Rankings - Road Running - Men and Women

Alan Mead

Ok. So we know we're on a hiding to nothing here in presenting the first set of Club Road Rankings for many a year. No doubt as this list is perused there will be mutterings of, "Oi! They haven't got my 2hours 17minutes for the Dog and Duck Half Marathon held last St. Swithin's Day." And of course, the mutterer will be right. The lists can only be based on the results we receive. So let us know of any additions by all means but next year make sure you keep us up to date with all your performancess. Send your results to Belgrave Rankings, [address withheld] or phone [phone number withheld].

And by the way, many thanks to Chas. Manning, Derek Crookes and Lionel Mann who have passed on the odd result sheets that they have come across.

Advertisement · page 75

BELGRAVE HARRIERS

Officially Sponsored by Tilda RICE

• Some of the greatest athletes in the world compete for Belgrave Harriers.

• Du'aine Ladejo (pictured over page) set the tracks of Europe alight in 1994, scorching around the 400 metre circuit to become European Champion. The powerful John Regis set a staggering British Record of 19.87 secs. for the 200 metres and a dozen others from our Club jumped, threw or ran at international level.

• We in Belgrave are proud of them all but so too are we proud of our slower men and women, girls and boys. As long as there are people prepared to "have a go", to enjoy the thrill of competition or a simple jog or walk around the roads, commons or parklands, our mission will always be to help them and show them how.

Belgrave Harriers was founded over one hundred years ago - in 1887 - and in those early days our headquarters actually were in Belgravia while our races were held along the Embankment of the River Thames and over the Common lands of South London.

Nowadays our members come from all around the globe but our spiritual home is still South London.

Battersea Park athletic track is where many of our track and field athletes train - come rain or shine, all the year round. We also use Kingsmeadow (near Kingston), Crystal Palace and various other stadiums.

At Wimbledon, just a couple of hundred metres from Wimbledon Common and available to all members, is our own Club Headquarters with separate changing facilities and showers for men and women, a refreshment room and gymnasium (for which a complete upgrade is planned).

We have coaches to assist you in whatever event you prefer but we are also looking for people who would like to help us to organise our club: timekeepers, judges, coaches, administrators . . . and then there are our social events.

Membership will cost you: £25* a year if you are over 20, £15* if you are 17 to 20 and £10* if you are under 17. Special rates exist for husband/wife or family membership. (* £20, £12 and £6 if you apply before 1st July 1995)

Main league events in which Belgrave Harriers compete: • Track & Field British League Division One - Senior Men. Southern Women's League Division Three - for ages 11 to adult. Southern Men's League Divisions Two and Six - for ages 15 to adult. Rosenheim League - for ages 15 to adult, men and women. Thameside League - for ages 11 to 14, boys and girls. Young Athletes (McDonalds) League - boys aged 11 to 16. National Junior League - boys aged 15 to 19. Southern Veterans' League - Men over 40 and over 50, Women over 35 and over 50. • Cross Country - Surrey Men's and Women's CC Leagues Division One - All age groups. • Road and Track Walking - many events - men and women all age groups.Road Running - Surrey Winter and Summer Leagues - men and women.

For more information about Walking or membership in general contact: The Membership Secretary, Carl Lawton, [address withheld]. Telephone [phone number withheld].

For information about Track & Field contact: Bob Bridges, [address withheld]. Telephone [phone number withheld] or meet him at Battersea Track (or ask for any Belgrave Harrier).

For information about Road Running or Cross Country contact: Gordon Biscoe, [address withheld]. Telephone [phone number withheld].

Find us at: Belgrave Hall, Denmark Road (off Ridgway), Wimbledon, London SW19 4PG. Telephone 0181 946 6859. on Tuesday and Thursday evenings at 6.30pm, Saturday afternoons at 2pm and Sunday mornings at 10.15am for distance running, jogging or walking, or at Battersea Park Athletic Track, Tuesday and Thursday evenings at 6.30pm.

Du'aine LadejoJohn RegisCarl LawtonBob BridgesGordon Biscoe
